The square of the Benedictines is the finest I ever saw; the cloisters have a flat roof & balustrade supported by double beautiful pillars wth rich capitells, a fountain also and statues in the middle, the corridors above stairs are long lofty & wide in proportion. N:B: we cou'd not see the library because the person who had the key was absent, the like pretext used in other places. prospect into the town and country very pleasant: each chamber of the fathers hath a noble balcony of stone / Corinthian & composite pilasters in front / the vast number of locusts / in the piazza the pillar from Brundisium supporting a statue in bronze of S: Orontius / cathedral handsome much gilding & indifferent painting, modern Architecture, noble steeples. Hospital rustic at bottom, double pilasters. Doric below, Ionic above, simple / seminary near the Cathedral rich facade, plain neat, handsome square within / Bishop's palace fine ascent by double stairs & balustrades, open arched portico. / facade of the Jesuites' church ornamented but not redundantly. as noble as I remember any where to have seen, very fine as likewise that of the Nosocomium S: Spiritus, very neat & unembarrassed, in wch Corinthian pilasters with festoon's between / houses generally but two stories, but noble air & well proportioned in height to the breadth of the streets / several fine gates nobly adorned / interdetto / people civil & polite, & so far as we had dealings, honest & reasonable / variety in the supporters of their balustrades / bold flights of Architecture, as in the facade of the church of S: Matteo a nunnery. / garlands and coronets often round their pillars and pilasters / church of the Carmelites very good, especially within, now building out of their own stock wch is onely 2000 ducats per annum & to maintain 26 persons. in the front a little diamond work wch they are sometimes guilty of / Dominicans a Greek cross, Carmelites whimsical unequal figure, others oval &c / no remains of Antiquity Lecce seems as large as Florence in extent but houses lower / not a spout or supporter to the balustrade, or balcony but wrought in the grotesque figure of some animal or otherwise carved / horses men griffins bears &c supporting the Balcony of the Benedictines church with a round window somewhat Gothic / stone handsome and well coloured / In no part of Italy

such a general gusto of Architecture / environs well inhabited / Gates Corinthian & composite / Jesuites convent vast building for 14 fathers / no river / their gusto too rich & luxuriant, occasion'd without doubt by the facility of working their stone / they seem to shew some remains of the spirit & elegant genius of the Greeks formerly inhabited these parts /
28 [May]
8 + 3/4 set out from Lecce; corn sheep pasture olives / olive grove / 10 + 25 quit the grove, corn, sheep pasture, fine view to the left of a country well inhabited white houses, extended fields, rows of trees, groves, scattered trees, the whole a wide plain / 11 + 10 corn wide unenclosed plain, few trees, reddish soil not very rich & somewhat sandy / 11 + 25, passed thro Guagniano a considerable village & well built. / stony road corn vines fig trees / stone walls for hedges / open stony ground / burnt grass as indeed everywhere / sheep a small flock / large vinyards right and left / wallnuts / spacious corn fields on Left, behind them trees and behind the trees a considerable town / corn right & left /. beans / 12 + 5 olive grove / corn & vines and walnuts & almonds mixed with the olive trees / got out of the grove at 12 + 40; olives & vines to the left, open country, corn and scattered trees on ye Right / flax / corn & olives Right & Left / 12 + 50 a wood, oaks and other forest trees thin, much underwood, oxen and cows, large birds like cranes. 1 + 20, quit the wood for a large plain cover'd with divers sorts of pretty green shrub & thyme whh we have often met with, and supply the place of the heath and fern / stubble goats & sheep right, corn right, shrub left / the country wide & flat, scattered trees & groves in view but no enclosures. stony field on the right, open pasture, sheep and oxen / corn / oxen / air perfumed with spearmint growing over an ample space right & left / 2 Bracciano a poor village where we dined under a fig tree by the side of a well in a poor man's garden who helped us to a sallad, &c this village belongs to the A:B: of Brindisi / 4 we set out from Bracciano /

THIRD JOURNAL (MS. 39309)
Large open plain in whh corn / shrub / corn, / pasture, cattle, goats, sheep / small ascent / shrub wide stony field / shrub & stony ground / long tract of corn interrupted in one place with a little flax in another with a few olives / rocky ground and corn on the left / road rocky corn right & left / parched pasture / ancient wall of huge uncemented stones grown rough with age on the right / 7 + 5 Casal-nuovo / Franciscan convent, treatment there / friar at midnight knocking at the door & singing / Thomas & Scotus, conversation with the guardian in Latin & another friar / Franciscans except Capucins not bitten or frightened [?] by the Tarantula those animals having been cursed by St. Francis / the habit worn 24 hours cures the Tarantato. 29 [May]
Walk out in the morning meet a Physician gathering simples in a field near the town, He judged the distemper of the Tarantati to be often feigned for lewd purposes &c as the spiritati / The wonderfull fountain whh being in a great subterraneous grotto runs into a cistern without ever filling it. In Salentino agro juxta oppidum Manduriam lacus ad margines plenus neque exhaustis aquis minuitur, neque infusis augetur. Plin. l. 2 c. 103. N:B: physician mistook Livy for Pliny. N:B: Idomeneus formerly took possession of the Ager Salentinus and inhabited them with a Greek colony. Thus Helenus by making [?] of them ports to Aeneas. Virg: Aen. Great remains of double walls of huge stones & fosse of the ancient Mandurium. The odd small old building consisting of a double rotunda & a large niche at the upper end and some walls as of a vestibule before it, said by the inhabitants to have been a temple of the Sun afterwards turned into a church, some old pictures of saints on the wall / seems built in ye early times of Christianity / Many if not most of the great stones in the old walls seem'd a composition of oyster & scollop shells intire cemented together by hard plaister / convents 6 & 1 nunnerie / 8000 souls tho I think over reckon'd belonging to the Prince of Francavilla / Corn, flax & cotton in

great plenty about Casal-nuovo / 7 + 50 left Casal-nuovo / corn olives left / few figs & wallnuts right, pasture amidst quarries, road very rocky, low shrubs & thyme, land open and poor / corn and figs for half a mile before we come to Oria / 10 + 5 Oria situate on a rocky hill, chain of small hills about 2 mile long and Oria on one of them. / a bishoprick / fragments of old pillars in the streets / goodly prospect to Gravina Brundisium Lecce &c. / inscription as follows on a pedestal lying in the churchyard of the cathedral D. M. COCCEIA M. F. PRIMA V. A. XX..... M. COCCEIUS FILIAE PIENTISSIMAE. / plain of vast extent round on all sides / part of an old Roman wall near the Castle / belongs to the prince of Francavilla / N:B: Several caves or grottos in a rocky hill near Uria / Oria belongs with the Ville of Moryins to the Prince of Francavilla. Oria anciently Uria most ancient city built by Cretans about the time of Minos. i.e. from after Strab: l. 6 Herod: 1.7 Set out from Uria at 1 after having dined wretchedly in a stable that being the only place we cou'd find in the towne, stony ground / corn & olives in abundance, figs, vines / long tracts of corn & long tracts of vines alternately, olives and fig trees, ditches on each side the road & bramble hedges / 2 + 1/2 grove of olives / ground gently wavy / 2 + 40 m quit the grove, large open tract of ground, stony field, spacious field of oats / stony road, shrub right, vinyard left / Francavilla about 2 miles on our right vines right and left / vinyard left, field of beans right / ridge of fruitful hills about two miles off on right / corn, beans / Rudiae the country of Ennius placed by Cluverius between Uria and Tarentum midway but we saw no ruines of that town. At Lecce they placed Rudiae within 2 or 3 miles of that city. this afternoon single houses up and down the country thicker than usual, few scattered trees throughout / pasture & stubble /. cows, oxen, sheep / corn & ciceri / stony field, plough'd land, corn / shrub on left, corn right / beans, corn / stones & shrub right / ample prospect of open country, pasture ploughed land &c bounded by gentle hills or risings / Get out of the spacious stony shrub / easy descent, olive grove / corn garden stuffe / Gulf of Taranto in view / large vinyard right & left / parched rough pasture / S: Giorgio a considerable town on our left / corn open / pass close by a village on our left / pasture & corn / rough, stony shrubby ground, flock of sheep almost all black the common colour in these parts / large shrubby, stony tract and corn &c a small distance to the right / slew a black serpent 4 feet long / plough'd land, corn, shrub /

7 came to the side of an arm of the Gulf on our right / great space of corn / olives at a distance to the left on a gentle hill / the ridge of low mountains still continued on the other side of the sea, / tufts of ciceri, rushes, olives, corn cows & oxen / ascent shrub, space of corn / corn, olives, vines / the olive trees large and many among the corn / vines & Fig trees / olives vines / & gardens, convents, houses / olives, pasture, corn left / convents & gardens right & left. / Arrived at the Zoccolanti Scalsi By 8 + 1/2. 8 + 3 open corn & Tarentum.
30 [May]
Taranto trade in corn and oil / inhabitants 15000 / no taste in the buildings / streets narrow and extream dirty / Abp's palace noble spacious apartments, loggie overlooking the whole Gulf of Tarentum / the security and noble prospect of that gulf. handsome seminary / near the Abp's palace logic Philosophy theology humanity taught in the same / youth secular & ecclesiastic are taught dieted and lodged for 30 ducats per annum each / N:B: These seminary's common / fine: inlaid chappel in the cathedral wch hath likewise ancient pillars in the great isle, with rude chapiters / various coloured marbles in the inlayings found in the ruines of the ancient city / nothing more beautiful than this oval inlaid chappel painted well enough above with the life of Saint Cataldus, an Irishman formerly A:B:p of Tarentum, now patron of the city, his body behind the great altar / The skull of S: Cataldo in the silver head (whh they say was finished by an angel) of his silver statue. His tongue also uncorrupted.
N:B: English Jesuites executed pictures in the convent at Lecce, put us out of countenance; English comforts, civilities, coach, present of Ham, wine. English cheese etc. / Caps of fishwool good for defluxions. Tide a remarkable thing here. Basilians here have some of the blood of S: Vitus in a glass viol wch being hard miraculously liquifies on the Saint's day.
A Gothic building shewn for Pilate's house / Several noble families settled in Taranto / Tarantato yt we saw dance here, no looking-glass or sword, stamped screeched seemed to smile sometimes, danced in a circle like the others / The Consul &c inform us that all spiders except the long-legged ones bite causing the usual symptomes tho not so violent as the large ones in the country / he tells me the Tarantula causes pain and blackness to a great space round the bite / thinks there can be no deceit. the dancing is so laborious / tells me they are feverish mad &

sometimes after dancing throw themselves into the sea and wou'd drown if not prevented / that in case the Tarantula be killed on biting, the patient dances but one year, otherwise to the death of the Tarantula / Ruines of old walls on the sea-shore half a mile from modern Tarentum, ruines of an Amphitheatre (different from what we had elsewhere seen as being without the passages) 1/4 of a mile from the town between the foresaid ruines and the town/ a mile from town the same way an old church and the grotto or subterraneous passage from the little sea to the gulf built of huge stones / all spiders except those with very long leggs & those in houses white & black / The taking of the Tarantula out with a straw nothing singular and done without whistling or spittle / Tarentum now in an island with two bridges / Two old columns of Verde antico in the chappel / The ruines of the amphitheatre defaced by the friars, who have a convent there & a garden in the amphitheatre / medals & intaglios found here / gold and silver wrought and unwrought, found along the side of the little sea which makes them believe the street of the goldsmiths shops was there / Corn wine oil fruits in abundance in the territory of Tarentum / Consul saies the Scorpion likewise causes dancing /
N:B: The Parthenians born in adultery during the war of the Lacedemonians against the Messenians... under the conduct of Phalanthus built Tarentum. being ashamed & having no interest to stay at home. N:B: the ancients as Strabo speak only of Tarentum as built in a low peninsula; hence the island made since by cutting of a... fortification. The little port about 12 miles round. N.B. the tongue being plain facilitated the Enterprise of Hannibal who advised the Tarentines to carry their fleet or drag it by land out of the little port into the sea; the Romans having the castle at the entrance of the port. See Livy.
Inhabitants of Taranto place their magazines of corn near the sea which insinuates it self thro the walls chiefly by the holes of the trabes & sending in a moist vapor swells the corn to 43 increase in the 100, to prevent it's rotting by this moisture they change it every 8 daies from one magazine to another / The experiment easily made by weighing equal bulks of theirs & the peasants' corn just brought to barn [?] This affirmed by the confessor [?] to the German fines viz 128

31 [May]
8 + 1/4 set out from Tarentum / The ancient Tarentum on a tongue of land between two seas same way by which we came towards Fagiano a town of the Albanian colony. / left our last road on the left olives & corn / & open corn fields / wide green wavy pasture, large flock of black sheep / No mountains in the heel of Italy. / Coarse pasture / open corn / all the way corn & pasture open country: hills at our left distant, sea near our right / N.B. Mistake in the mapps making the heel mountainous there being nothing more than gentle hills or risings & few of them / dined with an Albanian priest at Fagiano who treated us very civilly, he cou'd give no account of the first settling that colony, the men, he said, had been formerly employ'd in some wars of Italy & during their absence the women taking no care of their books they were destroyed so their M:SS histories and records perished / 1500 souls in Fagiano all Albaneses & speaking the Albanian tongue, their children learn the Italian at school / N.B. dress of the Albanesian women Fagiano a clean, irregular town instead of our thatch'd cabbins small square flat-roof'd white houses / The priest told us the arm e.g. being bitten by the tarantula swelled, confirmed, as indeed every body, that the common notion of the Tarantula's death curing the bite. / his house very neat, every where great respect for & knowledge of the English, owing to our commerce, fleets & armies / ancient Greek chappel painted with barbarous figures & inscriptions much defaced in characters partly Greek and partly barbarous / This priest never drank wine except at the Sacrament having an antipathy to it / Beside Fagiano la Rocca S: Giorgio & 3 or 4 more towns mostly Albanese, but Fagiano intirely / Bed of cuorioli or broken shells of periwinkles &c along the shore of the small sea used formerly as they sea [sic] in dying purple / wool in the fish called Baricella of wch stockings waistcoats &c like silk, but stronger / a little fish in the shell with the Baricella wch standing on the top of the open broad shell (the lower end being shaped like a horn & alwaies stuck in the ground) sees the approaching porpoise & retreating into the Baricella gives him notice to shut his shell / Three or four drops of oil spilt on the sea enables fishers to see the bottom / Abbate Calvo said Count Thaun had given 40000 pistoles for the continuation of his government the last year / a grain per rotolo tax on the beef/ the butchers discount with the town-collectors by little bits

of stamped lead given by the free persons for the tax of each rotolo / two islands in ye gulf that break the winds and make the harbor more secure / Taranto walled, a strong Castle, soldiers 128 /
June 1
1 + 1/4 set out from Taranto over the other bridge, corn / large grove of olives / corn mixt with olives being great old trees as indeed in every other grove / corn fields / corn apples olives pomegranates & other fruit trees / shrub & corn fields, a forest 1/4 of a mile distant left, Ridge of low fruitfull hills or risings all the way above a mile & a half, distant on our right / Town Matsafra on ye side of the said ridge The country we pass thro' plain & tho fruitful, hardly any houses to be seen / Dry'd pastures, unequal ground being descent / a small vale in whh tufts of rushes, olives figs &c ascent, a small village on left / cornfields plant'd with young olives in rows / long vinyards right & left planted with figs & other fruit-trees / poor pasture / corn right, olives left / a great open country not a perfect level, but nearly so consisting of pasture, corn & a vast large shrub of wild thyme &c / 5 + 35' ground wavy, some corn amidst ye shrub / N:B: the shrub lasted the greatest part of this daies journey / rugged stony ground, hills and vales mostly covered with shrub / 7 + 32' out of the shrub, corn-fields / grove of olives / inequality of hill and dale / ground rocky still olives / corn among the olives / quarry of white stone on ye right / wide corn-field on left / road hewn thro the rock / corn & olives on both sides / stone walls beans / 8 + 10' Castalneta / the people drawn up in the street in lines to see us / the number of clergy or abbates besides the regulars, these loiter in the streets particularly at Mandurium the Theatines / letter to the Dominicans from a clergyman at Taranto. their inhospitality in refusing to lodge us / we are receiv'd at the Capucins sit round their fire in the kitchen / Castalneta belongs to the prince of Acquaviva of a Genoese family / A bishoprick / 6000 souls / 3 convents of men and 2 of women / city dirty & nothing remarkable in art nature or antiquity / Odd to find the fame of Whig & Tory spread so far as the inland parts of South Italy & yet one of the most knowing fathers asked whether Ireland were a large town. library Scholastic & some expositors with a few fathers in a small room one or two classics / They take it ill to be asked if they have any poets. / in another convent they said what have we

to do with Virgil we want good sound books for disputing & preaching.
June 2
Set out at 7 + 12' the friars in a body accompanying us to the gate of the convent / land unequal, corn / vines figs almonds intermixed / corn / open country; large shrub to the left, pasture & few scattered fruit trees to the right / shrub on right & left / 8 + 50' get out of the great shrub into a spacious tract of wavy country or distinguish'd by risings / in it not a tree in view, some corn, some shrub, much the greater part stony pasture, a small brook / no cattle nor houses except one or two cottages occur in this ample space / sheep feed here in winter, in summer in the Abruzzo grass here being dry'd up in the summer & a fresh crop in September in the Abruzzo pinch'd with cold in the winter / These easy hills or rather risings & plains great mountains in ye maps / This immense region to the right & left à perte de vue appears desert, not a man nor beast / those who own the sheep mentioned are men of the Abruzzo many of 'em very rich & drive a great trade sending their wooll to Manfredonia & so by sea to Venice, their cheese to Naples & elsewhere up & down the Kingdom, they nevertheless live meanly like other peasants / and many with bags of money shant have a coat worth a groat / much cloth made at Venice / 10 + 40', grass deeper, white, yellow, red, blue flowers mixt with it / 10 + 55', vast opening before & on the right / on the left rocky hills / in all this vast tract not a tree or man or beast to be seen & hardly 2 or 3 scattered poor houses / an infinite number of butterflies & shrubs mixt with the pasture / 11 + 25' rocky ground / opening on right into a far extended green corn vale between green hills bearing corn to the very tops. rocky hills left / stony ground / a vale before with corn and vines & a few trees, the hills round have corn but no trees, except those on the right wch are barren & rocky without either trees or corn / pasture / wild corn, vines left / corn right, vines left for a long space / road cut thro the rock / inconveniently cold for several hours this morning / ciceri, vines, corn, great quarries in rocky hills on our left / few figs on left, corn on right / rocky ground / vines right & left / Matera 1 + 30 / archbishoprick / souls 17000 / they seem to misreckon being deceived by the figure of the towne / House 10 one above another like seats in a theatre Built down the sides of an oval hole / more men cannot stand on a mountain than on ye under plain / Dined in a garden offered by a farrier of the town as

we were looking for a tree in ye suburbs / the man very civil & well-behaved whh is the general character / Guardian of the Franciscan's letter to Gravina, he's displeased yt we stay'd not there in Matera as Calvo had intimated in his letter to him / Nothing extraordinary in the buildings or churches / all these inland townes in our return inferior to those on the Adriatic / 6 set out from Matera / vines corn walled gardens of fruit-trees, rocky road / wide opening descent / mostly high mountains at a distance on the left; hills before, pasture & corn / hills & vales all green / pasture, corn, shrub, the last but little & on the hills / vines left, corn, pasture / the same hilly country continued in the night / a world of shining flies / rocky hills / lost our way / arrived after much wandering afoot at a Franciscan convent without the walls of Gravina at 11 in ye night dark / Grana dat & vina clara urbs Gravina. inscribed over a gate of the town. last reckoning of the inhabitants 9850 / walled town / Dukes palace / bishoprick / Cathedral well paved with white marble / situate among naked green hills / 5 convents of men & 3 of women / unhealthy air in wet weather / Duke a wretch / Princes obliged by del Caspio to give their own or the heads of the Banditti with whom they went sharers / priests count the numbers of their parishioners at Easter / Bp of Gravina dead these two years, since whh no bishop in the town, the Viceroy not admitting the person made bishop by the Pope, as being a foreigner / N:B: The Bp of Matera 12000 crowns a year, these bishops not so poor as commonly thought / in Matera & Gravina they make a distinction between nobile and cavaliere the later being esteemed the higher rank
Basilicata contains ye greater part of ancient Lucania with some addition of the Magna Grecia & Apulia: produces good corn, oil, wax, limons, onions, croco [?], coriandolo. Justice & Finance administered in Matera. Besides wch Abp. ...9 bishops. Mountainous but pleasant. In it Matera, Venosa, Spinnazzuola [sic]. Dead above the living. canons 27 N.B. at Matera as we sate by a well in the garden on left appeared as there were tarantulas all round us.
June 3
Part from Gravina at 10; open green fields & hills mostly covered with corn backwarder than in the plain, corn the

commodity of the country / here & there rocky / rocky barren mountains about three miles distant on right / not a tree / some trees on our right thinly scattered / a small brook / Pasture & little corn 11 / great scene opening, long chain of barren mountains distant about 3 miles on right / open pasture, not a tree & pretty plain wavy rather than hilly, few blue mountains distant on left / a little corn on ye right thistles left / for half an hour passed a green vale of pasture, bounded with green risings right between our road & the stony mountains / 11 + 40 vast plain corn / the greater part pasture between ridges of mountains Apennine on the left old Vultur on the right / hardly a house on the plain or hills, the Vultur near & is a stony barren mountain / 1 + 20 a deep vale diversified with rising hills reaching to the mountains on left / 1 + 25 Poggio Ursini where we dined / chaplain lent us his chamber in the Duke of Gravina's Masseria, dirty / the Duke spends some time there in hunting / Tarantula not in this country, he hath seen several bitten with a black swoln mark as large as half-a-crown / they knew not they were bitten till dancing / tarantula bites onely in the hot months / a peasant at Canosa laughed at their biting & said he had often taken them in his hands / Duke of Gravina 30000 ducats per annum feudo & 30000 negotio / Doors & entrances of the houses dirty and forbidding here & elsewhere but otherwise at Lecce / 3 + 40 set out from Poggio Ursini along the same plain / pasture corn / beans left corn right / 4 + 10, descent into a vale pasture left meadow right with hay made / corn / plain pasture & green hills on right & left / after a little straying turn to the left & descend, tall thistles 5 foot high / corn in the vale / corn & pasture / 5 great length of corn along the bottom of the vale on the right / small hills & large spaces of rising ground well covered with corn & pasture. N:B: Italians living in townes makes 'em polite the contrary observable in ye English. Still between the mountains as before / ample space again / wood at a good distance on left of great length along the low mountains / 6 + 20 descend into a spacious plain, (not a perfect plain but rising land & vales intermixed) corn pasture & wood / not a house in view this afternoon / 6 + 3/4, Spennazzuola a village belonging to the Duke of Calabretta inhabitants about 3000, this seems too many for so small a place & yet I was assured it by a priest of the town / 3 convents / Situate pleasantly having on one side fine wood & hilly glens with trees & corn / on the other an open country corn & pasture / fleas innumerable.

N:B: City of Naples turbulent in Massianella's time & before in the governments of Cardinal Zagrati & the Duke of Otrunati [?]. Priest at Spennazzuola said tarantulas were in this country that not all spiders but certain ones of various colours in the fields were the true tarantulas, that they leave no mark the patient never knowing when [? where] he was bitten / he wou'd have it that Horace was an orator & no poet / Here was born the late Pope: it was then a Marquisate in the house of Pignatelli [?], but now belongs to the Tuttavilla family.
June 4
Set out at 6 + 3/4 open hills corn & pasture as before / corn / 7 + 1/4 large space of ground, shrub thin & pasture / forest trees on the right, ridge of woody mountains three miles on left / wide vale, shrub & pasture opening to the left displaying a delightful scene, a fruitful ridge of hills well wooded bounding the sight / 8 wood on right & shrub succeeding / Lopalozzo towne on a pleasant hill on the left, fruitful pleasant plain between over swelling hills & mountains on left / vale between gentle hills pasture corn shrub / rising ground corn / pasture & corn in a long vale on right, wood on the gentle hill that bounds it / rising land pasture shrub or copse, descent into an ample plain corn shrub pasture advancing obliquely to the woody mountains beyond whh higher mountains / delightful small vale environed with gentle hills most crowned with wood, a river or rather rivulet running through / 9 + 1/4 ascent little space through a wood, rising open corn field right wood left, beyond the corn on right pasture with cattel & beyond that chain of fruitful hills / up & down thro the skirts of a wood soil stiffe reddish clay, glade opening to the fruitful hills on right / 9 + 40 large corn field bounded with gentle hills a few scattered trees among the corn right forest left / down a hill at the bottom of which a rivulet, forest on both sides, long glade opening to the left bounded by the mountains / Left Acherontium now Cirenza on our left behind on a mountain's top / 10 + 25 Brionre a city on a mountain left & Barial on the mountain side large shrub being the skirt of the forest / a large plain shrub, pasture much corn in whh Venosa / all this while advancing obliquely to the mountains on the left / glyn large wallnut trees in the same descending road along the right side of it, bits of old walls on our right of the road / corn vines olives &c on the steep hills on either side, pass over a brook at bottom of our descent whh

stony / stony ascent after the brook / grottos on the left / the same glyn after turning now on right / arrived at Venosa at 12 / poor ill built town inhabited by peasants / souls 5000 / Bishoprick / churches mean / Statue of Horace being a sorry Gothic bust placed on the frieze [?] of a pillar in the place / Horatius Flaccus by name well known to all the poor men of the towne who flocked about to tell us on seeing us look at the statue / the men of this town in crouds gaping & following us about the towne, the idlest canaille & most beggarly I have any where seen / morsels of inscriptions in the walls pieces of pillars & other ornaments of rich marble about the streets / near the cathedral old brick walls shewn us for the house of Horace this say they we have by tradition. / by the fountain remains of 2 busts, with an inscription maimed underneath beginning C. Tullio / fine white marble lyon at the same fountain / Two or three more monumental stones with maimed epitaphs in a row. Venosa belongs to the prince of Tirella / 3 set out from Venosa whh is situate on a rising ground in a vale between the horns of the Apennine / (the horn on our right entering the town low & fruitful the Vultur anciently) / rising ground descent walnuts pomegranates olives figs vines corn / ascent fruit trees on right & left, corn & pasture & wavy plain / 4 along a narrow road between hills / thicket on either side / vale, brook on our left / stony road ascending, coarse narrow vale on the right bounded by stony or rocky hills / narrow between hills / vale opening to the right pasture much corn, herd of swine / Leucrienna / small river on the right running thro the vale / turn to the right thro corn part ripe & part reaped pass a stream / hills close on the left / vale with pasture & corn extended on the right / 6 + 1/4 narrower between hills, presently large opening ploughed land right corn left / not a house this afternoon / wide vale opening to the right & left / old church / green hills left partly covered with wood / corn reaped & ripe / two little houses near each other / river Aufidus in view on right running so as to make oblique angles with our road, his banks deep & shore spacious shewing him outrageous at certain times / His margin adorned with green trees / 7 + 1/2 crossed Aufidus, steep ascent, then a spacious plain corn / corn every where suffers for want of rain / wide pasture after the corn / flock of sheep black as usual / a straw cabbin belonging to one of the Abruzzo shepherds ascent, stony coarse pasture full of thistles not a tree / pasture less stony / Cappella small town on a rock distant 6 miles left / ample space of corn

right & left / 9 ascend out of the vale / N:B: all this day environed by mountains / after our ascent thro a difficult path, many ups & downs stony narrow & uneasy among shrubby mountains &c on foot we arrived in the night at an ample opening much corn & thence by an unequal stony road descended to the town of Ascoli where we arrived at 10 + 1/2 /. While on foot in the dark about 1/2 a mile before our chaises (whh we had lost & sought crying) we passed by some country folks eating beans in a field who kindly asked us to partake / Ascoli hath 500 fuoci / Bishoprick 10000 ducats / Duke of Ascoli residing there 15,000 duc p an: from tenants, besides 10000 from negotio / Roman bricks & fragments in the walls of houses, / several pieces of pillars, imperfect or defaced Roman inscriptions / grottos in the hill adjoyning. / Situation on a hill environed mostly by a plain, corn & pasture, not a tree hills on the east. Inhabitants are clergy & peasants / they boast of a saint's finger kept in a church of a convent on a hill overlooking the towne wch so far as the church is visible prevents the bite of the Tarantula / convents in Ascoli 3 / stone lions several here as at Venosa & Beneventum.
Venosa. This town a Roman colony and considerable republic. on a stone in the wall of the Cathedral I read this inscription: Lucullanorum mole Romana Aemilius Restituanus [?] V.P. Victor Apuliae & Calabriae in... splendidae civitatis........ conservavit.
Ascoli mentioned by Pliny, l. 1 c. 18. Dukedom, the Duke of the Muralli family; Bp suffragan to Beneventum.
5 [June]
Set out from Ascoli at 7, descent coarse pasture most some corn left / plain some corn much pasture, plain opening to the sea on right: 7 + 3/4 bridge over the Carapella / Villa cedri about 10 miles wide on left on a hill / ground dryed and burnt like a turf / N:B: Mornings cold afternoons hot / ascent convent on right, soon after descent some corn most pasture / soil burnt black / road black like turf / large parched plain continues bounded on each side by hills / 9 + 3/4 ascent then descent into a large vale: parched ground grass and corn, large grove of wild pear trees right; Troja on a hill before us / ascent: large

field of corn in a vale on right; better or less parched land than before. / Troja left on our right about 6 miles / 10 + 3/4 past a bridge over a perfectly dry'd stream / stony road thro woods / out of the wood hill covered with wood left shrubby hills on right / 11 + 20 Ponte Bovino / set out from Ponte Bovino or the great Inn at 2 + 3/4 / Stone road thro the Apennine on the side of the Cerbalus wh runs thro the bottom of the glyn on left / woody mountains right & left / Bovino city on the mountain top left / the deep vale or glyn on left full of trees / spots of corn now & then, as well in the vale on left as on the mountain on right / between whiles delightfull openings of cultivated land among bridge / Bauro town on ye mountain left / long bridge over a glyn / Monte Leone [?] towne on mountain right / another bridge dry / river now & then shews it self; large fountain built of square stone / pleasant shading from either hand across the road / 6 + 20 The mountains sink on either side & the road opens, the wood decreasing, fields of shrub & corn mixed therewith, on the sides of the mountain / flat slips of green corn along the bottom of the vale left / Bridge, wood ends in shrub / pasture & corn fields on a hill left / Savignano left Greci right; both on points of hills. Out of the shrub into an open hilly country Corn & pasture, bridge over a dry river not a drop of water / country grows more plain / wavy corn country / not a house to be seen / Hills fruitfull / 10 + 1/2 Ariano after several hours of windy rainy cold weather / forced to have a fire being exceeding cold (not wet) the 5th of June, N:S:
Troja is a fair city belonging to the Prince of the House of Avales [?]. gathers corn out of Apulia piaca [?] & vends it in great quantity in the Cajus.... I find it said in the Histories of the Kingdom that this city is thought founded d: anno 1008 by a colony of Grecians.
Cerbalus vulgo Cervaso. N:B from Taranto exclusive to Cervaso thro Peucetia wch name belongs to Apulia lying being Cerbalus and the isthmus (i.e. entrance of the heel) in ye full breadth to the sea. Inhabitants whereof were anciently called Dediculi. See Pliny l. 3, c. 11.
Ariano 12 parishes, 5 convents and a nunnerie.
June 6
8 + 25 left Ariano / descent large prospect of fruitful low hills covered with corn and trees like England R: and L: / Grove Left / delightful prospect of wide vale & chain of adverse hills

fruitfull / Furmini on a hill left / descent for some time past / rising hills fruitful yielding view like ye county of Armagh / Brook / Bonito on a fruitfull hill right / The other brook or branch of Fumorella / between Ariano and la Grotta wavy hilly open country corn & grass, some hills (especially [bis] about la Grotta and on the sides at some distance) well planted with trees, others bare of trees / little shrub near la Grotta. la Grotta at 11 / procession peasants in fine cloaths, host under canopy, firing guns, streamers & standards flourished, confraternities, clergy &c red & blue petticoats &c hung out for arras / N:B: a procession in the same place before / ascent between corn fields hills & vales thick scattered with trees / ascent thr enclosed road / on both sides fine gentle hills covered with corn & adorned with trees / all this day cold tho wrapt in my cloak / foggy mizling bleak weather like that in Ireland / beans, corn / ascent all the way from la grotta to Fricento / Shrub & corn, long view of pleasant hills left, long grove of oaks on pleasant rising ground right / ample fields on gentle hills fern corn oaks / deep glyn or vale full of trees left / another vale Right / beans, corn / oaks scattered all about / most ample prospect opening, hills partly wooded partly naked, towns on points of hills, Beautifull vales, elegant confusion / all this on looking to the north from a hill / In a sanctuary on Monte Virgine are contained the bodies of Shadrach Mesech & Abednego. This in the famous Monastery there resorted to for miracles & indulgences & reliques numberless. Stony road corn / top of a hill covered with fern / short descent corn / Jesualto in a vale right / vale of great extent running parallel to our road on right & terminated on the other side by mountains finely wooded & thrown together / Mons Tabor, anciently Mons Taburnus. From Fricento (where we dined sub dio without the towne in the view of many people) we went down a descent of three miles thro wood corn pasture to the Amsancti lacus / triangular whitish stinking about 40 paces about / Famiglietta threw in a dog who after half an hour came out bones / peasants find birds, hares, goats wolves &c dead about it. and go to look for 'em in the mornings during summer / 5 years agone 2 men found dead, the water good for the itch, wounds, leprosie / cold / thrown a yard high / other the like lakes but small / depth unfathomable / Silver all turned yellow whereas Vesuvius & Solfatara turn black / oaks smell being burnt / Small stream hard by the lake of a like whitish water / Stone hollowed at one end somewhat like a font. said to be,
a remain of the temple. / N:B: our Entertainment at Famiglietta's, &c.
N:B: commerce forbid with the Beneventans, they having lately coined 30000 ducts brass money and vended it in the Kingdom of Naples.
Fricento. The Aealanum of Ptolemy mentioned also by Cicero Epist in lib. 16 ad Atticum.
The temple of Mephitis by this lake according to Pliny. Tully also in Divinat. l. 1 mentions the lake.
June 7
Vale & beyond that vale, craggy, high, green, shrubby mountain / open fields, woods, fields planted with trees around / Vesuvio / townes & white houses scattered on the hills to the right, with Mons Taburnus / Amsancti valles to the left / this on looking to the west.--Pianura, campi Taurasini, Benevento lentano, flat ploughed land, wood in the middle / north--Trevico right, Ariano left, sea between naked mountains thrown variously together, villages ploughed land & woods in the vale Fiume Albi, east prospect--Amsancti valles two fine woods rising land between S. Angelo delli Longobardi right and la Guardia delli Longobardi left / high mountains to the right & left, lower before South Six bishopricks & 2 archbishopricks / Taurasi & la Torella / Fricento belongs to the Principe della Torella / 25000 souls [2500, verso] / July & 1/2 August without fires. An image on Monte Virgine protects the country about as far as visible from Tarantulas wch (say they) are here likewise. / two bears slain last year in a neighbouring wood / N:B: the hills near enough to be distinguished to be fruitful.
June 8
Set out from Fricento at 12, down hill corn pasture open / a few scattered trees / shrub left, corn deep vale right, before a vast opening vale between rising hills green yellow red different shades of corn fields with woods and scattered trees / lost the way among beans & corn / got into the great road / descent, rising hills, corn, woods; fruit-trees & few vines on either side the road / adverse long hill or fruitful mountain on the other side the Calore, Monte Mileto & Monte Fusco in the same / 6 left Ponte Calore, passed the river which in Italy is large enough /

ascent up a paved road, corn, pasture, trees, various rising ground / Monte Mileto left, on a hill covered with wood / vines twining round trees. left, corn & trees Right / vines hanging in festoons from tree to tree / Monte Fusco right / very good made road / immense prospect of vale & hills right, part wooded part not This view seen to advantage from Monte Fusco and Monte Mileto / our road like lightening. 8. got to the top whence a new extended scene discovered of vales and hills covered with wood likewise of high mountains & severall towns scattered on the sides and tops of hills, country beautiful, fruitful, various, populous very many new townes in delightful situations some on ye points of hills others hanging on precipices, some on gentle slopes &c Double most noble scene (just described both) seen from Monte Fusco lying to the eastward and westward / highest mountains right and left, covered with trees / Ponte del Prato large bridge, hardly a drop of water under it, hills and vales all round richly covered wth trees as well fruit as others & vines and spots of corn / another bridge over a valley for the convenience of travelling / Prato towne Right, ascent, descent long, bridge over a valley / cross a bridge over the Sabato 4 miles before we reach Avellino, shining flies / From Sabato we pass along an enclosed level road to Avellino, where we arrived at 10 + 1/2. Avellino reckons (I doubt misreckons) 30000 'tis an open, handsome towne situate in a vale among high mountains covered with wood. Fountain & towne-house adorned with busts and statues handsome enough / N.B. best inn I met wth in the kingdom here.
This country thick planted with townes. Avellino, Abellinum of Ptolemy. Houses have handsome decent façades, divers fountains & statues. Statue to K. Charles 2 of bronze on a fair pyramid in the middle of a large piazza part (adorning that piazza) of the public Granary or Storehouse well designed & adorned with many marble statues. Palace & gardens here of the Prince, who is of the family of Caraccioli. Situate at the root [?] of Monte Vergine in the Cathedral is preserv'd among other reliques a viol of the blood of S: Laurence wch liquifies on his feast day. thro this city goes the high road leading to five provinces hence the good Iron. The inhabitants numerous & singularly polite. manufactures of paper woollen cloth & iron, & it is said that the customs on the corn of Apulia passing thro this town brings into the prince 12000 ducats per annum.

N:B: from the other side of Ariano to pass Avellino in the Hirpini. N:B: From Avellino exclusive thro Campania Felix tho being left of the Hirpini.
Nola. This city a city of note & republic: after that a Roman colony. Qu. whether Augustus did not die here, as pretended by the natives who will also have it that anciently the sea came up hither from certain G..... Iron us'd as for fastening things. N:B: Augustini at Nola asked if the English were all Calvinists upon wch a discourse / Oxen & statues spoke in old Rome ye like facility in believing miracles now.
N:B: we were... of an Amphitheatre here, huge square stairs.
June 9.th
Set out from Avellino at 6 + 50 / a tall avenue of elms / Grove of Hasels (much esteemed here / on each side the road & vines in festoons from pole to pole among the nuts on left / avenue ends being a mile long / all this way on right & left high hills covered thick with trees chesnut or continued forest / large wall-nuts on the wayside / grapes in festoons on both sides / 8 + 1/4 hasels end / 8 + 20 pass thro Monteforte, a small towne, ascent, descent stony unequal road, between mountains covered with chesnuts close on either side / Hasels, walnuts, chesnuts all the way / vines in festoons, large cherries great number of trees thick laden with them all along the road / Hill on left almost naked having onely the stumps of trees / bridge / pass thro a village / vinyards in festoons right & left / village / vines & fruit-trees / another village / Figs, cherries, vines &c right and left / Village / 11 + 1/2 vinyards right & left / olives and vines left / vines right / (N:B: corn hemp &c among the vines for the most part) / vinyards right and left / I Nola souls, 3000, 7 convents men / 5 women
Pollio Julio Clementiano subventori civium necessitatis aurariae defensori, libertatis redonatori viae populi omnium munerum recreatori universa regio Romana patrono praestantissimo statuam collocavit.
Filice Sex. F Rufinae Sorori Fisi Sereni Aug. Larum Ministri. L D D D
Victoriae Aug. Augustales.
first inscription under a statue in the court of a private house / 2 other inscriptions under 2 of the 4 statues ancient in the place

before the Cathedral, one of the remaining two is of the same Pollius, the inscription of the other is defaced / The Bell / Bp 4000 crowns out of wch pension 2000 / left Nola at 3 + 3/4 / Thisus Alus Cujus &c over the Jesuites gate along the façade of the convent / apples plumbs, cherries / pears, apricots, vines, corn on each side the road / 4 + 1/4 festoon vinyards right & left also corn / Campagna between mountains Vesuvius left / 5 + 3/4 a village / still festoon vinyards, elms, corn right and left / but no mountains, at least none in view / 6 + 5 village / 6 + 3/4 village / N:B: The greatest part of this afternoon vines round elms without festoons 8 Naples.
Road from Rome to Naples.
1st post 6 miles, thro the flat campagna, some hay and corn, not a tree, hardly a cottage.
2d post to Marino 6 miles thro ye like flat campagna, tho ascending insensibly towards Marino, wch is a pretty, clean village belonging to the Constable Colonna.
3d post 9 miles, to Veletri. about 2 miles after Marino, pass by the lake of Castel Gondolfo on our right; view of Castel Gondolfo; land pretty well tilled in the beginning of this post. Within 3 miles of Veletri, steep descent to that city. This post over and among hills and woods.
This post we passed thro many alarms. We left Aricia now La Riccia about a mile on our right. N:B: Veletri, the ancient Velitrae the first town on the other side the Mons Albanus wch divided the country of the Volsci from Latium from Veletri to Terracina inclusive thro the country of the Volsci.
N:B: Horace went from Aricia to Forum Appii wch was situate down towards the sea on the pomptina palus drained then by Augustus over a canal cut from that towne to within three miles of Terracina. This he past in a boat, as was then usual. N.B. The Appian road went down to Forum Appii, thro the palus filled and dried by him so far as was necessary for making the road.
N:B: Near Terracina on ye Naples side a grotto with an entrance like a large door cut in the rock wch is also cut in the shape of the gable-end of a house.
4th post 8 miles & 1/2 first mile & 1/2 thro enclosures and trees.

7 last thro rising ground being spacious open green corn fields. Cisterna seat of the Prince of Caserta.
5th post 7 miles from Cisterna, the better part thro a forest with deer belonging to the Prince.
6th post 8 miles from Sermeneta lying thro the Campagna. a mile & 1/2 on ye other side Sermeneta attacked for a giulio. N:B: the Campagna green & in many parts woody, flat & marshy no houses, hardly any corn, no cattel, but a few buffaloes.
7th post to Piperno seven miles. Near a mile in the Campagna di Roma. the other 6 among hills and fruitful vales. Piperno situate on a hill.
8th post 8 miles. 2 first among wood and hills: 6 last thro a plain champaign, mostly uninhabited &c.
9th post to Terracina 8 miles, along the side of shrubby stony hills on left. Some ruins seeming of sepulchres on the road, on the right Monte Circello in view. All this post on right marshy low ground little cultivated or inhabited.
10th post to Fondi. 10 miles. limits of the Kingdom enter'd within 6 miles of Fondi. near 2 miles beyond the boundaries pass'd on our left a sepulchre of huge square stones very noble and entire now turned into a stable for asses. no inscription: the 2 first miles of this post close along the sea, being urged on the left by mountains. many broken rocks, as fallen in an earthquake on the road, about 5 miles further having woody & stony hills on left close and at small distance on right the palus Pomptina. land flat marshy hardly inhabited for the illness of the air. 3 last miles through a fruitful plain, oranges &c before we reached Fondi. A small river seemed to render it marshy and unwholesome flowing by the city on the side towards Rome.
11th post from Fondi to Itri 7 miles. first 3 or 4 miles over a plain gently ascending, planted wth cypress orange & limon trees near the towne of Fondi. last 3 miles between and over hills on the Appian way. these hills extend across to the sea.
These the Colles Formiani famous for producing good wine formerly. Mola a sea-port, poor towne. Formiae where the Formianum of Tully at which he was killed. They pretend to show the ruines of his house.
12th post from Itri to Mola 5 miles. Itri a towne poor & dirty but pretty large. This post enclosed between hills right and left, many olives, almost all on the Appian Way.

13th post from Mola to the Garigliano 8 miles. A large grove of olives after which, near 4 miles stony, unequal, shrubby ground, 4 miles more fine corn country meadows also pleasant and scattered trees in sight. Near the Garigliano we passed between an old aequeduct on the left and certain large ruines on the right, as of an amphitheatre.
These ruines of the ancient Minturnae a colony of the Romans. A few miles below Minturnae about the mouth of the Liris are situate the paludes Minturnenses where Marius hid himself up to the mouth in mud.
This post we had the mountains near us on left and sea on the right. divers ruines as seeming of sepulchres this post on the road side. greater part of this post on the Appian Way, whereof Fragments appear intire and ending abruptly as if part had been cut off or taken away. Liris larger than the Vulturnus. N:B: Treeto on a hill on the other side the aqueduct.
14th post from Garigliano to St Agata 10 miles. ferry over the river. Open large flat pleasant meadows along the Liris wch flowed on our left. after wch chain of mountains on our right, country unequal with pleasant risings, within 4 miles of S: Agata country thick planted with vines & olives, especially the latter of wch a perfect wood near S. Agata. N:B: Sessa fine town within less than a mile of St. Agata. Hence forward to Naples the Campania felix which begins either at the River Liris, or on the other side Sessa, the ancient Suessa Aurunca. N:B: from Fondi to ye Liris inclusive inhabited anciently by the Aurunci. Campania esteem'd the most delightfull part of the world by the ancients for the softness of the climate, quantity and quality of fruits & flowers, fertility of wine & corn....... hills cloathed with vines. Ancients full of passages to this purpose.
15th post from St Agata 10 miles. 2 first miles thro a country thick set with vines olives &c in wch the Appian way, no more of wch to Naples, hills these two miles on left and right at the end of these two miles a village where the left view of the Appian road. after this village a hilly country & great part of the road cut thro' a rock. then a wood of oaks, Cypress &c. after wch delicious country like the following post.
16th post 9 miles to Capoa thro' delicious green fields plain & spacious adorned with fruit-trees and oaks so scattered &

disposed as to make a most delightful landschape, much corn & fruit. Many white country houses beautifying the prospect; mountains on our left.
[NOTES ON THE GOVERNMENT AND POPULATION OF THE KINGDOM OF NAPLES]

(1) Principato citra, all Picenza with part of Lucania and Campania felix: its metropolis Salerno. Cities 18, whereof Salerno & Amalfi are A.Bps, the rest Bps. Grain & wine plenty.
(2) Principato Ulteriore provincia Hirpina with a small part of the land of the Samnites & Campanians; of 13 cities, 2, i.e. Beneventum and Conza, ABps, the rest Bps. Wine, chesnuts, hunting, fishing.
Terra di Lavoro 56990, besides Naples its casali, and about a 1/2 dozen more from towns whose fuochi are not numbered.

Gran corte della Vicaria supreme court like (somewhat) to our King's Bench: Governed by the Regent of the Vicaria a Cavaliere who, therefore is assisted by judges Civil & Criminal.
The great officers have the precedence, title & stipend due to their places. But their power is exercised by the King that of the Great Constable (i.e. Captain General) by the Generals collonels capitani d'armi &c that of the Gran Giustitiere by the Regent of the Vicaria. and in like sort of the rest.
Collaterale is the Supreme Royal tribunal composed of the seven great officers, the Consiglieri di Stato & the Regenti or of the 7 officers and Regenti della Cancellaria. This hath supreme power in making laws punishing magistrates commerce &c
Sacro Consiglio, consisting of President & Counsellors. Anciently the Kings of Naples appointed judges of appeal from the Vicaria & other Tribunals. But Alfonsus the first of Arragon took away those judges constituting this Sacro Consiglio di Giustitia to judge of appeals from all parts of the Kingdom. Not only causes of Appeal but likewise first causes are determined by them, for wch the President delegates such Counsellors to judge as he pleases. Their sentences are given in the King's name.
Regia Camera which takes cognizance of the Royal income or Patrimony (as they call it), i.e. taxes, customs &c in a word all that belongs to the Exchequer.
Gran corte della Vicaria above explain'd but this it's place.
So much from Capaccio what follows next from Pacichelli & others.
1mo. Tribunale is the Consiglio di Stato consisting of such persons as Viceroy pleases, a sort of Cabinet.
2º. Tribe: is the Collaterale consisting of six Regents of the cancellaria who have great power or rather sovereign in the management of affairs relating to civil institutions, commerce &c.
3º. Il Sacro Consiglio, un Presidente con Ventiquattro Consiglieri hears appeals & also first causes acts in the King's name.
4º. la Regia camera detta la Sommaria ha per capo il gran Camerlengo ma esercita la Giurisdittione per un Luogotenente scelto dal Rè. Under him are 8 presidents, Doctors, 3 presidents [?], idiots advocate, procurator fiscal, secretario, registers, accountants, clerks &c qui si maneggia il patrimonio reale &c si affitton Gabelle &c.

La gran Corte della vicaria si administra da un Luogotenente che si elegge ogni due anni dal Vicerè detto Regente. This court is divided into the two udienze civile e criminale 6 judges to each.
Divers other tribunals, as that of S. Lorenzo, governed by the eletti, 7 in number, but with 6 votes one being chose out of and for each Seggio, except that of Montagna, wch chooses two one for it self and one for Porcella a Seggio incorporated with it, but they have onely one voice.
N:B: The eletto del popolo is thus chosen. every ottina (of which there be 29 into wch the whole city is divided being the same with regions or wards) nameth two persons wch making in number 58 these assemble and with the Secretary of the Piazza del popolo form Revisori delli voti, after wch every of the 58 names being eletto, wch is often done with a malediction & invective scurrilous, si bossolano & si notano i voti and the six with most votes are written in a note and carried to the Viceroy (by 8 persons chosen by ballot out of the 58) who names wch he pleases, for eletto. The 58 likewise name a council of ten persons to assist their eletto. Every ottina likewise names 6 persons whereof the Viceroy chooseth one for Capitano of that ottina, who is a sort of Justice of peace taking care that no one offends or is offended in his ottina, take care of the poor, &c great power commanding so great a people.
The eletti of the Seggie are chosen different and left some longer shorter others in Court Seggie. N:B: the Eletti have the authority of the Seggios devolved on them by vertue of wch they act.
Capitani & eletti del popolo govern as long as the Viceroy or the Piazza pleases, but ordinarily for 6 months.
The power of the Tribunal of the eletti extends to setting a price on the annona. take care also of the health for wch they appoint two deputies one a noble the other a plebeian who govern a felucca that visits all ships boats &c and sees that nothing contagious enters the city. The eletti themselves pay a salary to these and give out patents for ships parting from Naples as likewise pay the man who watches to see the Quarintine duly performed & goods aired.
The Grassiero is a huomo Regio or magistrate appointed by the King. He was first joined to the council of the eletti in A.D. 1562 in the time of the Viceroy don Perafan di Ribera duke d' Alcalà, under the pretext only of providing the city with corn. but by little & little hath crept into all business & now in fact

is president of the Tribunal of the eletti who can do nothing without him.
Divers other tribunals or courts of lesser note as la Zecca Regia per pesi & misure: per li Notari per Dottori in legge & medicina &c &c
A Parliament or Deputation of 24 persons, 12 Deputati del Baronaggio & 12 della cità di Napoli. give a donative for wch effect use to be assembled by King's letter every 2 years. The city paies no part of these Donatives; yet the deputies of the city are the first to vote & subscribe and have precedence in all cases. but with this difference that the city hath but one vote, and the Baronaggio 12 6 titolatos and 6 plain Barons. Their use the Donative. These Deputies or Parliament meet in ye convent of S. Lorenzo. the Viceroy at the opening goes to have read the King's letter before the parliament by the Secretary of State. and at the close goes to receive their compliance with it.
Giulio Cesare Capaccio assures us that in his time the garden herbs eaten every month amounted to 30000 ducats in the city of Naples likewise that the gabella on fruit (it not being 1/2 of a farthing per pound of our measure & money) amounted or (wch is more) was set for 80000 ducats per annum, exclusive of oranges, limons, Bergamots & the like.
Four castles in Naples to protect & bridle the city. Castel St. Elmo, Castel-nuovo, Castel dell' ovo, and il Torrione del Carmine. N:B: The kingdom of Naples overrun or plundered by Goths; Saracens; Normans etc
Si ricavavano prima dal regno 5 milioni e più di rendita, oggi però se ne ritrahe da due millioni in circa. Pacichello. published 1703
The Nobility of the several parts or districts of the city of Naples were used anciently to assemble in certain public places or piazzas in each district where they conversed together. these places being much frequented they came to build certain open porticos; sustain'd by arches and railed round, thought in the reign of Carlo Imo who was an Angevino, i.e. in the 13th century see Capaccio. where they met together wch in process were improv'd and beautified in imitation of the Portici of the ancient Greeks and Romans and separated or appropriated to those families that used to assemble in them, and from being places of meer chat or conversation grew to be so many courts in wch they consider'd and debated on choosing magistrates and

providing for the health and plenty of the city. N:B: The Seggio's are five viz. il Seggio di Capoana, di Nido, di Montagna, di Porto, di Porta nuova.
N:B: there are divers or some very noble families in the city of Naples wch are not members of any Seggio.

FOURTH JOURNAL (MS. 39310)
image

Lac Virginis in Ecclesia S: Ludovici apud P.P. minimos Sti Francisci a Paulo asservatum liquefit quolibet assumptionis die.
Sanguis Johannis Baptistae liquefit quotidie in ecclesiâ quâdam Neapoli prout mihi referebat Dux quidam Neapolitanus.
Sbirri 150 tyrannized the island of Ischia cruelly, on account of seven persons who had slain one of their number. The relations to the number of 100 taken up and imprisoned at Ischia, general orders that no one remain in their houses in the country, all with their goods being obliged to repair to the towns. people met in the masserias beaten unmercifully. Fear and trembling and no going to do their business in their vinyards for 10 daies, then allowed to return some to their houses others not. Cellars of wine throughout the island all this while left wide open at the mercy of the Sbirri. Relations of the banditti seized in the churches. Some...... of the prisoners allowed the liberty of walking about the Fortress. The prisoners most part poor old women the men absconding and lying out of their houses in the woods for fear. Commissario della Campagna with his Sbirri continued about a month at Ischia. The inhabitants may

kill one another without fear of punishment, this rout [?] being never made but for the death of a Sbirro. We were alarm'd and roused out of our beds by 35 Sbirri one night.
The people of this island in other respects good enough, but blood thirsty & revengeful. Those of Foria & Moropane of worst fame for murdering, being said by the rest of the island to have no fear of god or man.
Castle of Ischia inexpugnable and as it were a Key to the kingdom. Hunting, fowling and fishing in Ischia. See Philostratus in imaginibus for Ischia also Capaccio in his Latin History of Naples. King Alphonsus built a Castle in the island, driving out the old inhabitants placed Catalans in their stead. Soliche [?] of Ischia... & prevalent..., but methinks very like over coats
The Habit of the Ischiots a blue scul-cap woollen, a shirt and pair of drawers,--in cold weather, doublet & breeches of wooll. They wear each by his side a broad pruning knife crooked at the end, with wch they frequently wound and kill one another.
Piano now Pieio, Casa Nizzola now Casamici, Fiorio now Foria.
A Fine plain all round Pieio planted with vines corn, & fruit trees.
The amphitheatre about a mile & half round the top. whence on all sides a shelving bank descends to the flat bottom, the wch bank cloath'd with oaks. Oaks, elms, chesnuts & cupe in this island. East of the Amphitheatre (wch is called la Vataliera vulgarly) is a village called Cumana & beneath a shaddy valley called il Vallone Cumano. between that village (seated on a mountain called il montte di Borano) and a high mountain called la montagna di Vezzi.
N:B Strange that Virgil sh'd call Prochyta alta & Statius call it aspera. Cluverius wonders yt Ovi<dius> sh'd speak of 3 islands wn there are but two. He also wonders Pythecusa sh'd be called sterilis wn Ischia is remarkably fruitful. the epithet sterilis therefore saith Cluverius rather belongs to Prochyta of wch Statius saith Haec videt Inarimen, illinc Prochyta aspera paret. Silv. l. 2., carm. 2. Now I wonder Cluverius sh'd talk thus there being 4 islands, & Prochyta very fertile & Pithecusa a barren rock. N:B: the great mountain wrongly called Epomeus by Jasolino, Strabo calling Epopeus. Strabo saith the island was inhabited by Chalcidenses & Eretrienses but that they were obliged to quit it by the earthquakes & fiery eruptions wch likewise

obliged certain persons sent by Hiero K: or Tyrant of Syracuse to quit certain works or buildings they had begun in the island. The word is τειχος. See Strabo l.5 where he likewise mentions a fiery eruption of Mount Epopeus a little before the age of Timaeus [?] on the Authority of that Historian.
Monte di Barano covered [?] with fair chestnut groves as much of the hills on the east side between yt and Ischia.
Pleasant vinyards overlooking Ischia on the middle between the two towns.
On the north side of the [bis] Cremate, about 2 mile long and 1 broad, fine hills covered with myrtle and lentiscus, vales too among them and towards the sea fruitful with vines &c hereabouts Pontanus formerly had a Villa. Onwards to the north-west you pass thro roads planted with myrtle &c vinyards & little inequalities of Hill vale wood, shrub &c to the Lake about a mile round on the border of wch the Bagno di Fontana.
Vista's in the island very various as sometimes in a plain thick planted with trees and vines, obstructing a distant view, at other times a patent prospect in a vale invironed with fruitful hills, on which white houses scatter'd. Borano with its steeple makes a pretty prospect being situate on a hill. Sometimes a deep road with high banks on either side, very refreshing in the heats. sometimes deep & tremendous precipices, many round hils gently rising, covered to the top with vines. sometimes horrid rocks and grottos, & clefts in the earth with bridges over them in some places.
The bath Ulmitello lies to the South part of the Island in a deep cleft between rocks, which opens into the strand of the sea. It is a well or two without buildings.
South of Testaccio there is a strange confusion of Rocks hills vales clefts & plains vinyards one above another jumbled together in a very singular and Romantic manner.
North or north west stands the Sudatorio di Castiglione in the side of a rock on wch Jasolino tells you may be seen the ruines of a Castle since the daies of Hiero. I saw some ruines of an old wall but nothing that looked like Greek or Roman work the stones and cement being but rude. I saw likewise the ruines of a Piscina or receptacle for water, well plaister'd: Between this rock and the sea in the vale lies Casa Cumana a small village where Jasolin thinks the Euboeans first inhabited. near the sea shore likewise in the vale I saw the bagno di Castiglione.

Two Eletti in the city of Ischia officers of the city supreme. When they go out of office they name each two candidates out of wch the eletti del popolo for next year are chosen by the Parlamento consisting of twenty persons 10 countrymen, ten citizens the wch parliament is new made reciprocally by the eletti as soon as they come into employment. This parliament consults of things relating to the well governing the town assessing taxes &c In Foria they have a Syndic for supreme magistrate likewise chosen by the people; there is another Syndic between Borano & Fontana. one year in Borano & names a Deputato to govern in Fontana & vice versa. This magistrate sets prices on meat, bread, corn wine &c. Catapani are inferior officers that go about the shops inspecting bread wine measures &c So far Signor Giam. Battista.
Jachino & Aniele say that once only in three years the Syndic is in each of the 3 following towns--Fontana, Borano, Casamici, the Syndic sending two deputati to the other places. Twenty men constitute the Senate of each of these 3 towns and Foria wch hath constantly it's own Syndic) these all vote for the eletti of Ischia. (who if I mistake not) reciprocally make the Syndics.
Several Gentlemen of Ischia taken up and sent some to be imprisoned at Naples, others at Sorrento, others at Caprea, at the same time that near 200 were imprison'd as relations of ye Banditti in the Castle of Ischia. These gentlemen were taken up on suspicion of having favour'd some-how the flight or concealment of them. Among the rest some of the eletti, Don Francesco Menghi, and Don Domenico Rinfreschi a man of great note were confined in their houses.
South-west of the island, on the sea shore near the Castle of St Angelo, is the arena of St Angelo, as also a hot bath. In some places a smoke & sulphurous smell issues from the sand. In others making a hole there suddenly issues out hot water wch in a little time, boils eggs beans or other things for the peasants.
Natale. saith there are forty in the Parliament of Ischia as many constitute that of Foria, 20 in the others. The eletti & syndics are proposed by the Marquis del Vasto or his Castellano double to the respective parliaments who chuse which they like.
The parliament men for life. Judge changed once a year.
Ischia, Campagnano, Pieio, Cumana, Testaccio, Borano, Fontana, Moropane, Pansa, Foria, Casamici, Cufa.
Inhabitants of Fontana keep flocks of sheep & goats. Lower parts of Monta S: Nicolo cloathed with vines, upper parts with

barley corn wheat, & Indian corn. top naked & white. Fontana situate among oak-trees. Narrow deep vales, like cracks in ye earth cloven by an earthquake, as appears by the opposite sides tallying as also from their shape: a bridge over one of these.
Foria in a plain situate at a corner of the island, having a sort of mole & harbor, the country about it full of vines & fruit-trees. Some rough land and ups and downs between that and Lo Lacco. this last town and Casamici situate among vines & fruit trees. after wch hills cover'd with myrtles & lentiscus, glyns groves of chesnuts &c.
The clergy of Ischia gett each a Caroline a mass. the Parish priest is not allowed to say above one Mass a day admits others into share of the profit arising from masses for the dead.
The number of the Clergy in Ischia accounted for by their lodging the goods of the Family in the name and under the protection of the priest who in case of murder or the like crimes secures 'em from forfeiture. The Bp admits none to orders who is not invested first with ye sum of 700 ducats.
Pontificum collegium usque ad Theodosii senioris tempora Romae fuit. Quibus uno edicto sacerdotum omnium reditus fisco applicati sunt. Zosimus.
Fat Quails in Ischia sold for 3 farthings a piece, these brought by wind from Africa hither and to Caprea whose Bishop's revenue consisting mostly of quails is uncertain as the wind.
Women imprison'd at Ischia as relations of ye Banditti after divers weeks set free at five ducats a head.
Quinces also and medlars in the island, and, among other fruits unknown to us two deserving note particularly viz. lazzeruoli & suorbi.
The inhabitants make a good deal of mony out of dry'd figs and Uvae passae.
Confraternity of 100 persons in Testaccio. when any one of these dies a hundred masses are said for his soul at the expence of the society it being a Caroline a mass. The like Fraternities all over the island as well as every where else in Italy. The parish priests fee is 7 carlines a death, a hen a birth, 15 carlines a marriage. On New years day, Easter day, Corpus Christi day he dispenses indulgences & all that are worth mony bring it him on these occasions according to their ability.
Mem: the celebration of St. George's (the patron of Testaccio) day & other Festivals.
Women's ornaments large gold earrings, and if married many

large gold rings set with false stones on their fingers, but the principal finery consists in the apron particolor'd and embroider'd with tinsel &c these worn only on holidays no more than the rings.
The ischiots likewise make presents of their wine & corn &c to the church, for supplying wax candles & keeping it in repair.
At certain times laymen go about begging money for buying wax candles. meeting 'em once on a time, I asked them for whom they sought charity. A woman standing by said for Jesus Christ.
Not a beggar to be seen in the island except now and then a poor Foreigner that comes to the baths.
No stories or notions of Ghosts among the common people.
In marriages of Ischiots, the Wedding day, the relations of the Bride Brothers sisters, &c accompany her to the Bridegroom's house (her father and mother excepted who alwaies stay at home) having left her there they return to the house of the bride's father and there sup, as the relations of the bridegroom do at his house. Next morning relations of both parties bring presents of Hemp, napkins, shirts, utensils for the house &c neatly done up in baskets to the house of the bridegroom where they are treated all that day at dinner.
In burials the fraternities accompany the corps. nearest relations mourn a month not shaving their beards for so long.
Burrhi [?] the Chymist told Sealy he cou'd do the miracle of S: Januarius's blood.
This Sealy is a lively old man that has eat 2000 vipers. I have seen him eat them raw and moving.
Si quis piorum manibus locus; si, ut sapientibus placet, non cum corpore extinguuntur magnae animae. placide quiescas, nosque domum tuam ab infirmo desiderio & muliebribus lamentis ad contemplationem virtutum tuarum voces, &c. Tacitus, in vita Agricolae. N.B. This like Papists praying to the dead.
N:B: The description given of the Bonzi in Japan by Maffeius Lib. 12 agrees to the Jesuites exactly, there being no such powerful & crafty institution among the old Romans as may serve to match them or be drawn into parallel.
3 or 400 ducats a common portion for a woman in Ischia.
Sept: 7 N:S: 1717
between 5 and six in the morning it began to thunder and continued without a moments intermission in one peal for the

space of above an hour during wch time the south sky seemed all on fire.
Quails said to be met in great numbers on the sea swimming with one wing up for a sail.
The Demoniacks of St Andrea della valle something like the foaming Priestesses or mad Bacchanals among the ancients.
Mem: to Consult V. Maximus for parallels to the Church of Rome.
Oranges, limons, olives, & medlars likewise grow in the island of Ischia.
Near relations as son e.g. on the death of his Father abstains two daies from all nourishment even a piece of bread or sup of wine. nothing but a cup of water.
Ischiots linnen all made of Hemp:
Urbe capta a Gallis. virgines vestales pedibus abeuntes L. Albinius in plaustrum recipit depositis inde uxore & liberis. Thinking of the English merchant at Leghorn who left his mother out of his will to leave all to the Jesuits or friars puts me in mind of this.
Sunday morning Sept: 19. N.S.
fair weather without rain wind or thunder. saw three flashes of lightening come into the chamber.
Children now as formerly brought to the temple of Romulus & Remus. Abbate Barbiere.
Roman matrons near 200 condemned for poissoning many principal persons of the city. Anno U.C. 424: of wch thing saith Livy prodigii ea res loco habita: captisque magis mentibus quam consceleratis similis visa.
Dictator made for striking a nail in ye wall of Jupiter's temple. Qu: if nothing like this in the Roman Church. Opening the gate at the Jubilee like this or the gate of Janus
Volsci Pontias insulam sitam in conspectu litoris sui, incoluerant. l. 9 D.1.
Mem: to Consult Dionysius Hallicarn. of the Roman religious rites & A: Gellius and Plutarch.
The H: Scriptures as formerly the books of the Sybills made a secret. The Oracles of Sybilla Cumana were kept in a stone chest in a cellar under the temple of Jupiter Capitolinus. inspected only by the Quindecimviri in cases of sedition, loss of battles, prodigies or the like when they directed how to proceed in expiating the Gods. Livy Dionysius &c Before ye 15 there

were ten & before them, two. Livy speaking of the Decemviri sacris faciundis calls them Carminum Sibyllae ac fatorum populi Rom: Interpretes.
Seculare carmen &c and the Jubilee:
Both honour their Deities with fine statues.
Both worship them with plays. Fireworks, music-meetings, comedies, letting off Guns are reckon'd Fine devotions in the Church of Rome. not omitting gaudy decorations of their churches possibly somewhat like lectisterniums.
Qu: whether as incense so wax candles were used by the Heathens.
The leaves of Myrtle & Lentiscus dry'd and sent to the tanners in Naples. Qu: about this & whether there may not a like use be made of leaves in England.
Mem: to mention ye statues on the tomb of Paul 3 & in ye church of the Carmelites.
Road between the lake and Ischia lying thro the remains of eruptions. the stones I saw among these remains, p.rly those worn under foot confirm the streets of Naples being paved with the matter of eruptions.
N:B: about five years since or less Mr. Littlejohn was present at a representation of our Saviours passion at the palace in Naples. it was a comedy horridly ridiculous as Judas acted best. They cried out Viva Giudas, etc. This was very shocking to some serious protestants present. Qu: whether the ancients did not as a piece of religion represent or act certain passages of the history of their fabulous Deities.
Strabo lib: 5. saith Procita was anciently broken off from Ischia. that the Eretrians & Chalcedonians (or people of Chalcis) were obliged to quit Ischia by earthquakes & eruptions of fire, of wch saith he there are many in the island: the same also obliged persons sent by Hiero to quit a building they had begun. Hence the Fable of Typhoeus, lying underneath it. He quotes Pindar as being of opinion that the whole tract of Italy being from Cumae and so on to Sicily is hollow'd underground with great caverns corresponding with each other. Hence Aetna, Vesuvius, Solfatara Ischia, liparean Islands burn, and that therefore he feigned Typhoeus to ly under that tract. He likewise quotes Timeus for horrible eruptions and earthquakes from Monte Epomeo wch caused even the inhabitants on ye coast of the continent to withdraw with fright into the midland parts of Campania. So far Strabo.

Pliny lib. 3. c.6 saith Ischia was called Aenaria from the good reception or station Aeneas' ships met with there & Pythecusae from the Greek pythos signifying an earthen pitcher. or sort of earthen vessel.
Ovid, Metam: l. 14
Inarimen Prochytamque legit sterilique locatas
Colle Pythecusas;
where Pithecusae & Inarime are plainly distinguished the former seeming to signifie only the town on the rock.
Mem: To consult Lucan lib. 5 & likewise for the Island Ischia.
It is observable that Livy too distinguishes Aenaria from Pithecusae. The same passage l.8. D.1 of Livy makes the Euboeans to have inhabited Ischia before Cuma wch Strabo says was the oldest city in Italy or Sicily. Hence Ischia the most anciently inhabited.
N:B: The promontory of Minerva of wch Pliny l.3 c.5 Surrentum cum promontorio Minervae Sirenum quondam sede. tho it had been rather in the island Caprea. One wou'd imagine the Syrens had lived on the barren rocks called Sirenum Scopuli near the promontory of Minerva, but yt of Apoll; and the following of Homer show the contrary.
Aloes and Indian figs grow wild in several parts of the island: at least the aloes grows wild. likewise dates, almonds, walnuts.
The Vista from S: Nicolo South Caprea and mountains beyond the bay of Salerno. South-east promontory of Minerva, & beyond that the cape of Palinurus vulg: capo di Palinuro, Massa, Vico, Surrento, Castelmare all on the side of a chain of mountains / East Vivaro, Procita Miseno, Baiae, Pozzuolo, Pausilypo, top of Naples or S: Elmo Vesuvius / Northeast Cuma / North Campania felice being to the sea a large plain on the other side bounded by mountains / Northwest Montes Massici (as I suppose) Mola, Caieta, a small isle &c. as far as the promontory of Retium. West Ponsa & two smaller isles more / Southwest the Sea.
